Each closure operator induces a topology and vice-versa (“version for children”)
Eduardo Ochs

Abstract
One of the main prerequisites for understanding sheaves on elementary toposes is the proof that a (Lawvere-Tierney) topology on a topos induces a closure operator on it, and vice-versa. That standard theorem is usually presented in a relatively brief way, with most details being left to the reader — see for example [ Joh77 , section 3.1] , [ McL92 , chapter 21] , [ LM92 , section V.1] , [ Bel88 , chapter 5] — and with no hints on how to visualize some of the hardest axioms and proofs. These notes are, on a first level, an attempt to present that standard theorem in all details and in a visual way, following the conventions in [ FavC ] ; in particular, some properties, like stability by pullbacks, are always drawn in the same “shape”. On a second level these notes are also an experiment on doing these proofs on “archetypal cases” in ways that makes all the proofs easy to lift to the “general case”. Our first archetypal case is a “topos with inclusions”. This is a variant of the “toposes with canonical subobjects” from [ LS86 , section 2.15] ; all toposes of the form 𝐒𝐞𝐭 𝐂 \mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{C}} , where 𝐂 \mathbf{C} is a small category, are toposes with inclusions, and when we work with toposes with inclusions concepts like subsets and intersections are very easy to formalize. We do all our proofs on the correspondence between closure operators and topologies in toposes with inclusions, and then we show how to lift all our proofs to proofs that work on any topos. Our second archetypal case is toposes of the form 𝐒𝐞𝐭 𝐃 \mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}} , where 𝐃 \mathbf{D} is a finite two-column graph. We show a way to visualize all the Lawvere-Tierney topologies on toposes of the form 𝐒𝐞𝐭 𝐃 \mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}} , and we define formally a way to “add visual intuition to a proof about toposes”; this is related to the several techniques for doing “Category Theory for children” that are explained in the first sections of [ FavC ] .

1
Subobjects and inclusions

The
subobjects
of an object
D
of a topos
\mathbf{E}
are the
monics with codomain
D

modulo isomorphism
. Here is an example
in
\mathbf{Set}
:

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{h}
\textstyle{\{20,40\}}
\textstyle{\{2,4\}}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}20\mapsto 2\\
40\mapsto 4\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\{20,40\}}
\textstyle{\{2,4\}}
\textstyle{\{2,4\}}
\textstyle{\{2,4\}}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}2\mapsto 4\\
4\mapsto 2\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\{2,4\}}
\textstyle{\{2,4\}}
\textstyle{\{20,40\}}
\textstyle{\{1,2,3,4\}}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}20\mapsto 2\\
20\mapsto 4\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\{2,4\}}
\textstyle{\{1,2,3,4\}}
\textstyle{\{2,4\}}
\textstyle{\{1,2,3,4\}}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}2\mapsto 4\\
4\mapsto 2\end{smallmatrix}}

Here the monics
f:A\rightarrowtail D
,
g:B\rightarrowtail D
,
h:C\rightarrowtail D

are all equivalent; in some texts they are “the same subobject”.
Let’s make that precise. For us the elements of
\operatorname{Sub}(D)
are the
monics with codomain
D
. If
(f:A\rightarrowtail D)
,
(g:B\rightarrowtail D)

are elements of
\operatorname{Sub}(D)
then they are
equivalent
(notation:

f\equiv g
) iff there is an iso
A\leftrightarrow B
making the obvious triangle commute.
We write
[f]
for the equivalence class made of an
f\in\operatorname{Sub}(D)
and
all other monics in
\operatorname{Sub}(D)
equivalent to
f
, and we write

\overline{\operatorname{Sub}}(D)
for
\operatorname{Sub}(D)
modulo equivalence: so

[f]\in\overline{\operatorname{Sub}}(D)
.

A monic
g:B\rightarrowtail D
in
\mathbf{Set}
is an
inclusion
if it obeys:

\forall b\in\operatorname{dom}(g).\,g(b)=b.

The usual way to formalize inclusions in toposes is via canonical
subobjects. A topos
\mathbf{E}

has canonical subobjects
is it comes
equiped with a class
\mathsf{CanSub}(\mathbf{E})
of monics that obey a certain
list of properties — see
[
LS86
, p.200 onwards]
— that
are also obeyed by the inclusions in
\mathbf{Set}
. Here we will do something
similar but with a different list of properties, and in section

3
we will see how to translate our proofs, done
in toposes with inclusions, to proofs in arbitrary toposes.

When
f:A\rightarrowtail C
and
g:B\rightarrowtail C
are subobjects of
C
we
say that
f
is contained in
g
(notation:
f\subseteq g
) when there is
a monic
m:A\rightarrowtail B
making the obvious triangle commute. We call

m
the “mediating map”.

In
\mathbf{Set}
we have two different operations that take two maps
f,g

with a common codomain and produce pullbacks:

\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\{\,(a,b){\in}A{\times}B\;|\;f(a){=}g(b)\,\}}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{\pi^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\{\,(a,b){\in}A{\times}B\;|\;f(a){=}g(b)\,\}}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{\{\,a{\in}A\;|\;f(a)\in B\,\}}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{\{\,a{\in}A\;|\;f(a)\in B\,\}}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{f}

The second one only works when the right wall is an inclusion, but it
produces pullbacks whose left walls are inclusions. In both cases we
will write the left wall as
f^{-1}(g):f^{-1}(B)\rightarrow A
,

\textstyle{f^{-1}(B)}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{f^{-1}(B)}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{f^{-1}(g)}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{f}

and there will be no default name for the top wall. When the right
wall is marked as an inclusion we will use the second pullback
operation, otherwise the first one.

In
\mathbf{Set}
the classifying map of a monic
m:A\rightarrowtail B
is defined
as:

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\chi_{f}:=\\
(\lambda b:B.\exists a\in A.m(a)=b)\end{smallmatrix}}

and the “true” map
\top:1\hookrightarrow\Omega
is the inclusion
\{1\}\hookrightarrow\{0,1\}
.

The
inclusion classified by a map
f:B\rightarrow\Omega
is the map

f^{-1}(\top)
; we will sometimes write it as
\sigma(f)
. Note that for any
monic
m:A\rightarrowtail B
we have
m\equiv\sigma(\chi_{m})
, and we have
m=\sigma(\chi_{m})

if
m
is an inclusion; and for any
f:B\rightarrow\Omega
we have
\chi_{\sigma(f)}=f
.

1.1
Inclusions, precisely

A
topos with inclusions
is a topos
\mathbf{E}
endowed with a class
of monics
\mathsf{Incs}(\mathbf{E})
, called the
inclusions
, and two
pullback operations, as in the previous section, obeying the
properties below:

Inc1)

For any two object
C
and
D
of
\mathbf{E}
there is at
most one inclusion from
C
to
D
. When that inclusion map exists
we write it as
C\hookrightarrow D
— we don’t need to name it — and we
say that
C
is a subset of
D
(notation:
C\subseteq D
).

Inc2)

Each
[f]\in\overline{\operatorname{Sub}}(D)
contains exactly one inclusion
map. This can be expressed as

\textstyle{\forall A}
\textstyle{\exists!B}
\scriptstyle{\exists!}
\textstyle{\forall A}
\textstyle{\forall D}
\scriptstyle{\forall f}
\textstyle{\exists!B}
\textstyle{\forall D}
\scriptstyle{\exists!g}

in the variant of Freyd’s diagrammatic language defined in

[
FavC
, section 4.1]
. We will say that this
g
is
the
inclusion associated
(or:
equivalent
)
to
f
, and write
this as
\mathsf{can}(f)=g
.

Inc3)

The composite of two inclusions is an inclusion. Or, in
the language of Inc1: if
B\subseteq C
and
C\subseteq D
then
B\subseteq D
, with
B\hookrightarrow D=B\hookrightarrow C\hookrightarrow D
.

Inc4)

If
f:B\hookrightarrow D
and
g:C\hookrightarrow D
are inclusions with

f\subseteq g
then the mediating map
m:B\rightarrowtail C
is an inclusion. In
the language of Inc1:
f\subseteq g
implies
B\subseteq C
. We can visualize this as:

\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{g}

Inc5)

The “true” map
\top:1\hookrightarrow\Omega
is an inclusion.

Inc6)

The second operation that produces pullbacks in
\mathbf{E}

receives maps
f:A\rightarrow C
and
g:B\hookrightarrow C
and returns pullbacks
whose left walls are inclusions. In a diagram:

\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{f^{-1}(B)}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{f^{-1}(B)}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{f^{-1}(g)}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{g}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{f}

Inc7)

The
intersection
of two inclusions
B\hookrightarrow D
and

C\hookrightarrow D
is defined as their pullback:

\textstyle{B{\cap}C}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{B{\cap}C}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{D}

Note that its upper wall is the mediating map from the composite

B{\cap}C\hookrightarrow C\hookrightarrow D
to
C\hookrightarrow D
, so it is an inclusion.

Using Inc2 we can see that
B{\cap}C
and
C{\cap}B
are the
same

subset of
D
, not just isomorphic subobjects.

We write
\mathsf{Incs}(D)
for the class of inclusions with codomain
D
and

\mathsf{Subsets}(D)
for the class of subsets of
D
. In a topos with
inclusions we have:

\mathsf{Subsets}(D)\cong\mathsf{Incs}(D)\cong\overline{\operatorname{Sub}}(D)\cong\operatorname{Sub}(D),

where the first two ‘
\cong
’s are isomorphisms and the last one is just
an “equivalence of categories”: if we start with a monic
f
in

\operatorname{Sub}(D)
, take it to its equivalence class
[f]
in
\overline{\operatorname{Sub}}(D)
,
and then go back to
\operatorname{Sub}(D)
, what we get is
\mathsf{can}(f)
, and we have

f\equiv\mathsf{can}(f)
but not necessarily
f=\mathsf{can}(f)
.

1.2
Inclusions in a topos of the form
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{C}}

From here onwards
\mathbf{C}
will always denote a small category.

All toposes of the form
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{C}}
are toposes with inclusions. We define
the class of inclusions of
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{C}}
,
\mathsf{Incs}(\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{C}})
, as follows:

IncSC)

A morphism
i:A\hookrightarrow B
in
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{C}}
is an inclusion iff
for every object
u
of
\mathbf{C}
the map
i_{u}:A(u)\hookrightarrow B(u)
is an
inclusion in
\mathbf{Set}
; that is, if
\forall u\in\mathbf{C}.\forall a\in A(u).i_{u}(a)=a
.

Take an inclusion
i:A\hookrightarrow B
and a morphism
v:u\rightarrow w
in
\mathbf{C}
. As

i
is a natural transformation, these squares commute:

\textstyle{u}
\textstyle{w}
\scriptstyle{v}
\textstyle{A(u)}
\textstyle{B(u)}
\scriptstyle{i_{u}}
\textstyle{A(u)}
\textstyle{A(w)}
\scriptstyle{A(v)}
\textstyle{B(u)}
\textstyle{B(w)}
\scriptstyle{B(v)}
\textstyle{A(w)}
\textstyle{B(w)}
\scriptstyle{i_{v}}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{{B(v)(a)}}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{A(v)(a)}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{B(v)(a)}
\textstyle{A(v)(a)}
\textstyle{A(v)(a)}

This means that
\forall a\in A(u).A(v)(a)=B(v)(a)
– so
A(v):A(u)\rightarrow A(w)
is a
restriction of the function
B(v):B(u)\rightarrow B(w)
to
A(u)
.

1.3
‘And’ and ‘implies’

In section
2.2
we will need the “internal conjunction
map”,
(\land):\Omega\times\Omega\rightarrow\Omega
, whose internal view is
(P,Q)\mapsto P\land Q
, and
the “internal implication map”,
({\rightarrow}):\Omega\times\Omega\rightarrow\Omega
, that works as
(P,Q)\mapsto(P{\rightarrow}Q)
. They are well explained in sections 13.3 and 13.4 of

[
McL92
]
, but only in their forms “for adults”, that work in
arbitrary toposes. In this section I will just complement

[
McL92
]
by showing briefly how those definitions that hold in
any topos are translations of definitions that make sense in
\mathbf{Set}
.

The arrow
(\land)
is built as the classifying map of the inclusion

\sigma(\land)
in this diagram,

\textstyle{\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\land Q\,\}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\land Q\,\}}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(\land)}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\land:=\chi_{\sigma(\land)}}

and the inclusion
\sigma(\land)
is built as an equalizer. We have:

\begin{array}[]{l}\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\land Q\,\}\\
=\;\;\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P=\top\land Q=\top\,\}\\
=\;\;\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}(P)=\top_{\Omega}(P)\land\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}(Q)=\top_{\Omega}(Q)\,\}\\
=\;\;\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;(\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}\times\mathrm{id}_{\Omega})(P,Q)=(\top_{\Omega}\times\top_{\Omega})(P,Q)\,\}\\
=\;\;\mathsf{Eq}((\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}\times\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}),(\top_{\Omega}\times\top_{\Omega}))\\
\end{array}

\textstyle{\mathsf{Eq}((\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}\times\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}),(\top_{\Omega}\times\top_{\Omega}))}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{eq}((\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}\times\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}),(\top_{\Omega}\times\top_{\Omega}))}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}\times\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top_{\Omega}\times\top_{\Omega}}
\textstyle{\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\land Q\,\}}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\sigma(\land)}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{(P,Q)\mapsto(P,Q)}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{(P,Q)\mapsto(\top,\top)}

Where the map
\top_{\Omega}
is defined as:

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{!_{A}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top_{A}:=\top\circ!_{A}}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{1}
\scriptstyle{!_{\Omega}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top_{\Omega}:=\top\circ!_{\Omega}}

The arrow
({\rightarrow})
is the classifier of the inclusion
\sigma({\rightarrow})
, that is
built as another equalizer:

\textstyle{\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\rightarrow Q\,\}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\rightarrow Q\,\}}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\sigma({\rightarrow})}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{({\rightarrow}):=\chi_{\sigma({\rightarrow})}}

\begin{array}[]{l}\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\rightarrow Q\,\}\\
=\;\;\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;\top\leq P\rightarrow Q\,\}\\
=\;\;\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;\top\land P\leq Q\,\}\\
=\;\;\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\leq Q\,\}\\
=\;\;\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P=P\land Q\,\}\\
=\;\;\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;\pi(P,Q)=(\land)(P,Q)\,\}\\
=\;\;\mathsf{Eq}(\pi,\land)\\
\end{array}

\textstyle{\mathsf{Eq}(\pi,\land)}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{eq}(\pi,\land)}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\pi}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\land}
\textstyle{\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\rightarrow Q\,\}}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\sigma({\rightarrow})}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{(P,Q)\mapsto P}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{(P,Q)\mapsto(P\land Q)}

Note that in
\mathbf{Set}
we have:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\land Q\,\}&=&\{(1,1)\},\\
\{\,(P,Q)\in\Omega\times\Omega\;|\;P\rightarrow Q\,\}&=&\{(0,0),(0,1),(1,1)\}.\\
\end{array}

2
Closure operators

A
closure operator

\overline{(\cdot)}
on a topos with inclusions
\mathbf{E}
is
a family of operations like this,

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\overline{(\cdot)}_{E}:&\mathsf{Incs}(E)&\rightarrow&\mathsf{Incs}(E)\\
&(d:D\hookrightarrow E)&\mapsto&(\overline{d}:\overline{D}\hookrightarrow E),\\
\end{array}

where we have one
\overline{(\cdot)}_{E}
for each object
E
of the topos, and
these ‘
\overline{(\cdot)}_{E}
’s obey:

C1)
d\subseteq\overline{d}
,

C2)
\overline{d}=\overline{\overline{d}}
,

C3)
c\subseteq d
implies
\overline{c}\subseteq\overline{d}
,

C4)
\overline{c\cap d}=\overline{c}\cap\overline{d}
,

C5)
\overline{f^{-1}(d)}=f^{-1}(\overline{d})
,

for all inclusions
c:C\hookrightarrow E
and
d:D\hookrightarrow E
and for
all maps
f:B\rightarrow E
.

We will draw the properties C1, C2, C3, C5 as:

\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{\overline{\overline{D}}}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}
\textstyle{\overline{\overline{D}}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\overline{d}}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c}}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}

\textstyle{f^{-1}(D)}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{\overline{f^{-1}(D)}{=}f^{-1}(\overline{D})}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{f^{-1}(D)}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{f^{-1}(d)}
\textstyle{f^{-1}(D)}
\textstyle{\overline{f^{-1}(D)}{=}f^{-1}(\overline{D})}
\textstyle{\overline{f^{-1}(D)}{=}f^{-1}(\overline{D})}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f^{-1}(d)}{=}f^{-1}(\overline{d})}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}

Where all the ‘
\rightarrowtail
’s in the diagrams are inclusions.

Important:
in all diagrams from this section to section to

2.6
all the ‘
\rightarrowtail
’s will stand for inclusions.
This is for typographical reasons, to make the diagrams a bit lighter.
The distinction between ‘
\rightarrowtail
’s and ‘
\hookrightarrow
’s will reappear in
section
3
.

2.1
Topologies

A
(Lawvere-Tierney) Topology
on a topos
\mathbf{E}
is a map
j:\Omega\rightarrow\Omega

obeying:

LT1)
j\circ j=j
,

LT2)
j\circ\top=\top
,

LT3)
j\circ\land=\land\circ(j\times j)
.

We draw LT1, LT2, and LT3 as:

\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\land}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j\times j}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\land}

One way to grasp the intuitive meaning of LT1, LT2, and LT3 is to look
at their internal views. If we have maps
p,q:A\rightarrow\Omega
, the internal views
of

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{p}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\langle p,q\rangle}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\land}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j\times j}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\Omega\times\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\land}

are:

j(P(a))
as

; we will develop this
idea in section …. .

2.2
Topologies induce closure operators

Theorem 2.2.1.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a topos with inclusions, and let
j
be a topology on
it. For each inclusion
d:D\hookrightarrow E
let
\overline{d}:\overline{D}\hookrightarrow E
be
the inclusion that is classified by
j\circ\chi_{d}
, as in the diagram below:

\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{d}}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}

Then this operation
d\mapsto\overline{d}
is a closure operator —
i.e., it obeys C1, C2, C3, C4, C5.

Proof.

For C1, rename the second 1 to
1^{\prime}
in the diagram above and draw the
identity map
1\rightarrow 1^{\prime}
. The slanted rectangle with
\overline{D}
in its
upper left corner is a pullback. We can factor the maps
d:D\rightarrowtail E
and
!:D\rightarrow 1^{\prime}
through it,

\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{1^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}
\textstyle{1^{\prime}}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{d}}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1^{\prime}}

and this gives us a mediating map
m:D\rightarrowtail\overline{D}
. It is easy
to check that this
m
is a monic and an inclusion.
1
1

1

I thank
David Michael Roberts for helping me with this.

For C2, draw the diagram below:

\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\overline{\overline{D}}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\overline{\overline{D}}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\overline{d}}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{\overline{\overline{D}}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{d}}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}

The inclusion
\overline{d}
is classified by
j\circ\chi_{d}
and
\overline{\overline{d}}

is classified by
j\circ j\circ\chi_{d}
. By LT1 we have
j\circ j=j
, and so
j\circ\chi_{d}=j\circ j\circ\chi_{d}
. This means that
\overline{d}
and
\overline{\overline{d}}
are two
inclusions classified by the same map — so
\overline{d}=\overline{\overline{d}}
, and the inclusion
\overline{D}\hookrightarrow\overline{\overline{D}}
is the
identity.

To prove C4 we use the diagram below and the series of equalities at
the right of it:

25.29814pt\raise-18.2397pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63542pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j\times j}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 44.97528pt\raise-30.14609pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.84357pt\raise-37.4794pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.45468pt\raise-17.82304pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.63542pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{j}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 82.45468pt\raise-30.14609pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 30.86415pt\raise-37.4794pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega{\times}\Omega}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.84357pt\raise-37.4794pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\Omega}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 61.77054pt\raise-32.53494pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-1.94446pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\land}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 75.84357pt\raise-37.4794pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}}}\quad\begin{array}[]{rcl}\chi_{(\overline{c\cap d})}&=&j\circ\chi_{c\cap d}\\
&=&j\circ\land\circ\langle\chi_{c},\chi_{d}\rangle\\
&=&\land\circ(j\times j)\circ\langle\chi_{c},\chi_{d}\rangle\\
&=&\land\circ\langle j\circ\chi_{c},j\circ\chi_{d}\rangle\\
&=&\land\circ\langle\chi_{\overline{c}},\chi_{\overline{d}}\rangle\\
&=&\chi_{(\overline{c}\cap\overline{d})}\\
\end{array}

The inclusions
\overline{c\cap d}
and
\overline{c}\cap\overline{d}
are classified by the
same map, so they are equal.

The proof of C3 is this series of inferences:

\mathstrut\overline{c}\subseteq\overline{d}\mathstrut\overline{c}=\overline{c}\land\overline{d}\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut\overline{c}=\overline{c\land d}\mathstrut c=c\land d\mathstrut c\subseteq d\mathstrut\overline{c\land d}=\overline{c}\land\overline{d}

The proof of C5 is this diagram

\textstyle{f^{-1}(D)}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\overline{f^{-1}(D)}}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{d}}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}
\textstyle{f^{-1}(D)}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{f^{-1}(d)}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{f^{-1}(D)}
\textstyle{\overline{f^{-1}(D)}}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\overline{f^{-1}(D)}}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{\overline{f^{-1}(d)}}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}

plus these equalities:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\chi_{(\overline{f^{-1}(d)})}&=&j\circ\chi_{f^{-1}(d)}\\
&=&j\circ\chi_{d}\circ f\\
&=&\chi_{\overline{d}}\circ f\\
&=&\chi_{f^{-1}(\overline{d})}\\
\end{array}

The inclusions
f^{-1}(d)
and
f^{-1}(\overline{d})
are classified by the
same map, so they are the same inclusion.

2.3
Restricting a
\overline{(\cdot)}_{E}

In this section we will see how a closure operation
\overline{(\cdot)}_{E}
can be
“restricted” to a subset
D\subseteq E
.

Theorem 2.3.1.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a topos with inclusions, with a
closure operator
\overline{(\cdot)}
. If
C\subseteq D\subseteq E
in it, then the closure of
m:C\hookrightarrow D
can be calculated from the closures of
c:C\hookrightarrow D
and
d:D\hookrightarrow E
— and we have
\overline{m}=d^{-1}(\overline{c})
and
\operatorname{dom}(m)=\overline{C}\cap D
.

Proof.
draw the diagram at the left below, that is the diagram
for C5 with some things renamed. The pullback of
c:C\hookrightarrow E
and
d:D\hookrightarrow E
is
C\cap D
, which is
C
; so
\overline{d}^{-1}(c)=m
, and we
have the diagram at the right.

\textstyle{d^{-1}(C)}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\overline{d^{-1}(C)}}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}

\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{d^{-1}(C)}
\textstyle{D}

\textstyle{\overline{d^{-1}(C)}}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d^{-1}(c)}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{C^{D}{=}\overline{C}{\cap}D}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{C}

\scriptstyle{m}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{C^{D}{=}\overline{C}{\cap}D}
\textstyle{C^{D}{=}\overline{C}{\cap}D}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{\!\!\overline{m}=d^{-1}(\overline{c})}
\textstyle{C}

\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{E}

Our notation for the domain of the closure of an

when

\overline{C}
is taken will be

, for “the closure of

in

”; the operation ‘

. As

is the pullback of

and

we have

.

Theorem 2.3.2.

Let
\mathbf{E}
be a topos with inclusions with closure
operator
\overline{(\cdot)}
. If
D\subseteq E
in
\mathbf{E}
, then
\overline{(\cdot)}_{D}
can be obtained
from
\overline{(\cdot)}_{E}
in the following way:

\begin{array}[]{rrcl}\overline{(\cdot)}_{D}:&\mathsf{Incs}(D)&\rightarrow&\mathsf{Incs}(D)\\
&(m:C\hookrightarrow D)&\rightarrow&(\overline{m}:C^{D}\rightarrow D)\\
&&&:=(d^{-1}(\overline{c}):\overline{C}\cap D\hookrightarrow D)\\
\end{array}

where
c
is
c:C\hookrightarrow E
and
\overline{c}
is its closure,
\overline{c}:\overline{C}\hookrightarrow E
.

Proof.
This is an easy corollary of Theorem

2.3
.

2.4
Dense and closed

For the next theorems we need some definitions:

An inclusion
c:C\rightarrow D
is
dense
iff
\overline{c}=\mathrm{id}_{D}
.

An inclusion
d:D\rightarrow E
is
closed
iff
\overline{d}=d
.

Theorem 2.4.1.

If an inclusion
a:A\hookrightarrow B
is dense and closed then it is the
identity.

Proof:

\mathstrut a=\mathrm{id}_{B}\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut\overline{a}=\mathrm{id}_{B}\mathstrut a\text{ dense}\mathstrut\overline{a}=a\mathstrut a\text{ closed}

Theorem 2.5.2.

The map
j
defined above is a topology.

Proof.
To prove LT1 we have to see that
j=j\circ j
. We have

\overline{d}=\overline{\overline{d}}
for all inclusions
d
; so
\chi_{\overline{d}}=\chi_{\overline{\overline{d}}}
always. We have
\chi_{\overline{d}}=j\circ\chi_{d}
and

\chi_{\overline{\overline{d}}}=j\circ j\circ\chi_{d}
, so
j\circ\chi_{d}=j\circ j\circ\chi_{d}
always holds.
There is a way to make
\chi_{d}=\mathrm{id}
here — which is when
d:D\hookrightarrow E

is
\top:1\hookrightarrow\Omega
— and so a particular case of
j\circ\chi_{d}=j\circ j\circ\chi_{d}
is

j\circ\mathrm{id}=j\circ j\circ\mathrm{id}
, which gives us
j=j\circ j
.

To prove LT2 we have to see that
\top_{\Omega}=j\circ\top_{\Omega}
, i.e., that

. To do this we draw this diagram,

\textstyle{\Omega}

\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}

\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1}

and check that its two upright squares and its three lower slanted

.

To prove LT3 we start by choosing any two inclusions with the same

and

. From the maps

\textstyle{\Omega{\times}\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\land}
\textstyle{\Omega{\times}\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega{\times}\Omega}

We have

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\land\circ(j\times j)\circ\langle\chi_{c},\chi_{d}\rangle&=&\chi_{\overline{c}\cap\overline{d}}\\

and C4 tells us that

2.6
A bijection

We saw that a closure operator induces a topology and that a topology
induces a closure operator. Now we need to check that these two
operations, that we can abbreviate as
\overline{(\cdot)}\mapsto j
and
j\mapsto\overline{(\cdot)}
, as below,

\textstyle{\overline{(\cdot)}}
\textstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{j:=\chi_{\overline{\top}}}
\textstyle{\overline{(\cdot)}}
\textstyle{j}
\scriptstyle{\overline{(\cdot)}:=(\lambda d.\sigma(j\circ\chi_{d}))}

are inverses to one another — i.e., that the composites

\overline{(\cdot)}\mapsto j\mapsto\overline{(\cdot)}
and
j\mapsto\overline{(\cdot)}\mapsto j
are
identity maps. We will organize all this visually as:

\textstyle{(\lambda d.\overline{d})}
\textstyle{\overline{(\cdot)}}
\textstyle{\overline{(\cdot)}}
\textstyle{\chi_{\overline{\top}}}
\textstyle{(\lambda d.\sigma(\chi_{\overline{\top}}\circ\chi_{d}))}
\textstyle{\chi_{\overline{\top}}}
\textstyle{j\circ\chi_{\top}}
\textstyle{\chi_{((\lambda d.\sigma(j\circ\chi_{d}))(\top))}}
\textstyle{(\lambda d.\sigma(j\circ\chi_{d}))}
\textstyle{\chi_{((\lambda d.\sigma(j\circ\chi_{d}))(\top))}}
\textstyle{(\lambda d.\sigma(j\circ\chi_{d}))}
\textstyle{j}

To prove that
\overline{(\cdot)}\mapsto j\mapsto\overline{(\cdot)}
is the identity we need
to check that in any topos with inclusions with a closure operator

\overline{(\cdot)}
we have that
\overline{(\cdot)}
, i.e.,
(\lambda d.\overline{d})
, is equal to

(\lambda d.\sigma(\chi_{\overline{\top}}\circ\chi_{d}))
. It is enough that check that we have

\overline{d}=\sigma(\chi_{\overline{\top}}\circ\chi_{d})
for any inclusion
d
. Look at the
diagram below…

\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{J}
\textstyle{J}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{E}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{d}}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{\overline{\top}}}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{\overline{D}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{d}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{J}
\textstyle{J}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\overline{\top}}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}

To prove that
j\mapsto\overline{(\cdot)}\mapsto j
is the identity we need to
check that in any topos with inclusions with a topology
j
we have

j=j\circ\chi_{T}
. Look at the diagram below:

\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{1}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{\top}}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{j}

We have
\chi_{\top}=\mathrm{id}_{\Omega}
, and so
j\circ\chi_{T}=j\circ\mathrm{id}=j
.

3
Translating all this to toposes without inclusions

Let’s start by an example – we will translate Theorem

2.3
.
This diagram condenses the two diagrams of the original proof into a
single one:

\textstyle{d^{-1}(C){=}D{\cap}C{=}C}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{C^{D}{=}\overline{d^{-1}(C)}{=}d^{-1}(\overline{C}){=}D{\cap}\overline{C}}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{d^{-1}(C){=}D{\cap}C{=}C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}d^{-1}(c)\\
=(d\cap c)^{\prime}\\
m\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{d^{-1}(C){=}D{\cap}C{=}C}
\textstyle{C^{D}{=}\overline{d^{-1}(C)}{=}d^{-1}(\overline{C}){=}D{\cap}\overline{C}}
\textstyle{C^{D}{=}\overline{d^{-1}(C)}{=}d^{-1}(\overline{C}){=}D{\cap}\overline{C}}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{\phantom{mmmmm}\overline{m}=\overline{d^{-1}(c)}=d^{-1}(\overline{c})=(d\cap\overline{c})^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c}}

where
(d\cap c)^{\prime}
and
(d\cap\overline{c})^{\prime}
are maps in intersection pullbacks.
The convention is that if
a:A\rightarrowtail C
and
b:B\rightarrowtail C
are
monics then the components of the diagram for
A\cap B
are named like
this:

\textstyle{A\cap B}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{(a\cap b)^{\prime\prime}}
\textstyle{A\cap B}
\textstyle{A}
\scriptstyle{(a\cap b)^{\prime}}
\textstyle{A\cap B}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle(a\cap b)}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{b}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{C}
\scriptstyle{a}

This is how I would start to structure the proof above to implement it
in a proof assistant. Most nodes in this tree

\mathstrut C^{D}=D\cap\overline{C}\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut C^{D}=\overline{d^{-1}(C)}\mathstrut C=d^{-1}(C)\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut d^{-1}(C)=D\cap C\mathstrut d\text{ inclusion}\mathstrut D\cap C=C\mathstrut C=D\cap C\mathstrut C=C\cap D\mathstrut C\subseteq D\mathstrut\overline{d^{-1}(C)}=d^{-1}(\overline{C})\mathstrut d^{-1}(\overline{C})=D\cap\overline{C}\mathstrut d\text{ inclusion}

state that two inclusions with different constructions are isomorphic,
and so they are the same morphism. For example, “
C=d^{-1}(C)
” is an
abbreviation for this:

(m:C\hookrightarrow D)=(d^{-1}(c):D^{-1}(C)\hookrightarrow D)

The properties of inclusions let us omit the codomains and the names
of the arrows in many cases, and write only their domains.

We can regard the tree above as a
proof
of this
equality

of
inclusions
that appears at the root node:

(\overline{m}:C^{D}\hookrightarrow D)=((d\cap\overline{c})^{\prime}:D\cap\overline{C}\hookrightarrow D)

We can translate it to a
construction
of this
isomorphism

of
monics
:

(\overline{m}:C^{D}\rightarrowtail D)\equiv((d\cap\overline{c})^{\prime}:D\cap\overline{C}\rightarrowtail D)

Now the names of the morphisms are primary and the names of the
objects secondary. I prefer write both, otherwise I feel that the
translated tree becomes unreadable. Here is the translation of the
upper left part of the previous tree:

\mathstrut(\overline{m}:C^{D}\rightarrowtail D)\equiv({\overline{d^{-1}(c)}:\overline{d^{-1}(C)}\rightarrowtail D})\mathstrut(m:C\rightarrowtail D)\equiv(d^{-1}(c):d^{-1}(C)\rightarrowtail D)\mathstrut(d^{-1}(c):d^{-1}(C)\rightarrowtail D)\equiv(m:C\rightarrowtail D)\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut(d^{-1}(c):d^{-1}(C)\rightarrowtail D)\equiv(\mathrm{id}\cap m:D\cap C\rightarrowtail D)\mathstrut d\text{ monic}\mathstrut(\mathrm{id}\cap m:D\cap C\rightarrowtail D)\equiv(m:C\rightarrowtail D)\mathstrut(m:C\rightarrowtail D)\equiv(\mathrm{id}\cap m:D\cap C\rightarrowtail D)\mathstrut(m:C\rightarrowtail D)\equiv(m\cap\mathrm{id}:C\cap D\rightarrowtail D)\mathstrut(m:C\rightarrowtail D)\subseteq(\mathrm{id}:D\rightarrowtail D)

I tried to draw a diagram with all the morphisms in the tree above
following my usual conventions, and I found the result too messy. But
if we translate the original diagram to this,

\textstyle{d^{-1}(C){\equiv}D{\cap}C{\equiv}C}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{C^{D}{\equiv}\overline{d^{-1}(C)}{\equiv}d^{-1}(\overline{C}){\equiv}D{\cap}\overline{C}}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{D}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{d}
\textstyle{d^{-1}(C){\equiv}D{\cap}C{\equiv}C}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}d^{-1}(c)\\
\equiv(d\cap c)^{\prime}\\
m\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{d^{-1}(C){\equiv}D{\cap}C{\equiv}C}
\textstyle{C^{D}{\equiv}\overline{d^{-1}(C)}{\equiv}d^{-1}(\overline{C}){\equiv}D{\cap}\overline{C}}
\textstyle{C^{D}{\equiv}\overline{d^{-1}(C)}{\equiv}d^{-1}(\overline{C}){\equiv}D{\cap}\overline{C}}
\textstyle{D}
\scriptstyle{\phantom{mmmmm}\overline{m}\equiv\overline{d^{-1}(c)}\equiv d^{-1}(\overline{c})\equiv(d\cap\overline{c})^{\prime}}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{c}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{\overline{C}}
\textstyle{E}
\scriptstyle{\overline{c}}

and we define in the right way how to interpret the ‘
\equiv
’s in it, then
everything works. In

\textstyle{A_{1}{\equiv}A_{2}{\equiv}A_{3}}
\textstyle{B_{1}{\equiv}B_{2}{\equiv}B_{3}{\equiv}B_{4}}
\scriptstyle{f_{1}{\equiv}f_{2}{\equiv}f_{3}{\equiv}f_{4}{\equiv}f_{5}}

the “object”
A_{1}{\equiv}A_{2}{\equiv}A_{3}
means that we have three objects with
known, but unnamed, isos between each one and the next, like this:

A_{1}\leftrightarrow A_{2}\leftrightarrow A_{3}
, and the “arrow”
f_{1}{\equiv}f_{2}{\equiv}f_{3}{\equiv}f_{4}{\equiv}f_{5}
is
in fact five “isomorphic” arrows, and each
f_{i}
goes from some

A_{j}
to some
B_{k}
, but the diagram does not say what are these
‘
j
’s and ‘
k
’s; in this context “the ‘
f_{i}
’s are isomorphic”
means that the diagram made by
A_{1}\leftrightarrow A_{2}\leftrightarrow A_{3}
,
B_{1}\leftrightarrow B_{2}\leftrightarrow B_{3}\leftrightarrow B_{4}
, and all the ‘
f_{i}
’s commutes.

The translation sketched above works for all constructions and proofs
in sections
2
–
2.6
. It may be possible to
characterize the class of constructions and proofs on which it works,
but this is far beyond the scope of these notes.

4
Toposes of the form
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}

In sec.
1.2
we conventioned that
\mathbf{C}
would always
denote a small category. In this section we will need many other
conventions like that, especially to define Grothendieck topologies in
sections
4.6
and
4.7
.

From here onwards
\mathbf{D}
will always denote a finite DAG regarded as
posetal category. We will consider that
\mathbf{D}
is downward directed:
if
u,v\in\mathbf{D}
then we say that
u
is
above

v
, or
v
is
below

u
, when we have an arrow
u\rightarrow v
. We will say that
u
is

strictly above

v
when we have an arrow
u\rightarrow v
and
u\neq v
. Note
that every
u\in\mathbf{D}
is above itself.

In contexts in which we have a category
\mathbf{D}
our (default) topos
will be the topos
\mathbf{E}=\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
, with the inclusions given by the
definitions in sec.
1.2
. We will use the conventions
from
[
FavC
, section 7.12]
to draw its objects; for example, if

\mathbf{D}=\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)=\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-10.0,19.0)\put(10.0,46.667){\vector{10}{-9.333}{10.0}}\put(0.0,47.0){\vector{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(30.0,47.0){\vector{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(0.0,28.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${1\_}$\hss}}\put(0.0,56.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${2\_}$\hss}}\put(30.0,28.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\_1}$\hss}}\put(30.0,56.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\_2}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)

then these are two objects of
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
:

A=\left(\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 10.50002pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-5.5pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern There is an inclusion
f:A\hookrightarrow B
between them.

We will use a notation with expressions like ‘
(a\in A(u))
’s to make
certain calculations easier to visualize. For example,

\begin{array}[]{rrl}A\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\_2\\
\downarrow\\
\_1\end{smallmatrix}\right)(4\in A(\_2))=(7\in A(\_1))&\text{will mean}&A\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\_2\\
\downarrow\\
\_1\end{smallmatrix}\right):A(\_2)\rightarrow A(\_1)\\[7.0pt]
&\text{and}&A\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\_2\\
\downarrow\\
\_1\end{smallmatrix}\right)(4)=7,\quad\text{and}\\[10.0pt]
f(4\in A(\_2))=(4\in B(\_2))&\text{will mean}&f(\_2):A(\_2)\rightarrow B(\_2)\\
&\text{and}&f(\_2)(4)=4.\end{array}

Note that the way to expand the expressions at the left is different
for functors (first case) and for natural transformations (second
case).

4.1
The Heyting Algebra
H

A
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
has several diffent terminal objects. The symbol 1, or

1_{\mathbf{E}}
, will by default denote the one that has
1(u)=\{*\}
for
every
u\in\mathbf{D}
. Its class of subsets always forms a set; for example,
when
D=\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)
we have:

\mathsf{Subsets}(1_{\mathbf{E}})=\left\{{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}},\;{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}},\;{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}},\;{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}},\;{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}},\;{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}},\;{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right\}

Note that here the small ‘0’s mean ‘
\emptyset
’ and the small ‘1’s mean
‘
\{*\}
’. We will use the notations for piles from
[
PH1
, sec.15]

to draw this as:

H\;=\;\mathsf{Subsets}(1_{\mathbf{E}})=\;\;\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-1.5,-0.5)\put(-1.0,3.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${21}$\hss}}\put(0.0,4.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${22}$\hss}}\put(-1.0,1.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${10}$\hss}}\put(0.0,2.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${11}$\hss}}\put(1.0,3.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${12}$\hss}}\put(0.0,0.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${00}$\hss}}\put(1.0,1.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${01}$\hss}}\put(2.0,2.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${02}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}

but with a difference: in
[
PH1
, sec.15]
two-digit numbers are
interpreted as subsets of the set of points of the current default
two-column graph — so, there we would have
21=\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}2\_,&\\
1\_,&\_2\end{smallmatrix}\right\}\subset\mathbf{D}_{0}
— while here they will be subsets of
1_{\mathbf{E}}
; so, here
this holds:

21={\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}={\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-8.0,12.0)\put(8.0,28.56){\vector{9}{-6.12}{9.0}}\put(0.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(25.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(0.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{*\}}$\hss}}\put(0.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{*\}}$\hss}}\put(25.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{*\}}$\hss}}\put(25.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\emptyset}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}\subset{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-8.0,12.0)\put(8.0,28.56){\vector{9}{-6.12}{9.0}}\put(0.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(25.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(0.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{*\}}$\hss}}\put(0.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{*\}}$\hss}}\put(25.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{*\}}$\hss}}\put(25.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{*\}}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}=1_{\mathbf{E}}\;.

In contexts in which we have a category
\mathbf{D}
the letter
H
will
denote this set
\mathsf{Subsets}(1_{\mathbf{E}})
, regarded as a Heyting Algebra,
and we will refer to its elements as
truth-values
. In contexts
in which our
\mathbf{D}
is a 2-column graph this
H
will be a
Planar
Heyting Algebra — in the sense of sections 4 and 17 of

[
PH1
]
— and we will denote these truth-values by two-digit
numbers.

4.2
Some definitions on posets

Suppose that
\mathbf{P}
is a downward-directed poset; we will denote its set
of points as
\mathbf{P}_{0}
. A subset
\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbf{P}_{0}
is a
down-set
iff it obeys
this:

\forall u,v\in\mathbf{P}_{0}.\;\left(\begin{matrix}u\\
\text{above}\\
v\end{matrix}\right)\rightarrow\left(\begin{matrix}u\in\mathcal{A}\\
\downarrow\\
v\in\mathcal{A}\end{matrix}\right)

We will denote the set of all down-sets of
\mathbf{P}
by
\mathsf{D}(\mathbf{P})
, and if

\mathcal{A}\subset\mathbf{P}_{0}
then we will denote the down-set generated by
\mathcal{A}
— i.e.,
the smallest down-set of
\mathbf{P}
containing
\mathcal{A}
— by
{\downarrow}_{\mathbf{P}}\mathcal{A}
, or by

{\downarrow}\mathcal{A}
. When
u\in\mathbf{P}_{0}
we will usually write
{\downarrow}\{u\}
as
{\downarrow}u
; so,
if

\mathbf{D}=\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)=\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-10.0,19.0)\put(10.0,46.667){\vector{10}{-9.333}{10.0}}\put(0.0,47.0){\vector{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(30.0,47.0){\vector{0}{-1}{10.0}}\put(0.0,28.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${1\_}$\hss}}\put(0.0,56.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${2\_}$\hss}}\put(30.0,28.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\_1}$\hss}}\put(30.0,56.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\_2}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)

then:

\begin{array}[]{rr}&{\downarrow}_{\mathbf{D}}\{1\_,\,\_2\}={\downarrow}_{\mathbf{D}}\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}&\_2,\\
1\_&\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right\}=\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}&\_2,\\
1\_,&\_1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right\},\\[5.0pt]
&{\downarrow}_{\mathbf{D}}\,2\_={\downarrow}_{\mathbf{D}}\{2\_\}={\downarrow}_{\mathbf{D}}\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}2\_&\\
&\phantom{1\_,}\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right\}=\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}2\_,&\phantom{1\_,}\\
1\_,&\_1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right\}.\end{array}

We will use the poset
\mathbf{D}
above in all examples in this section.

Every
{\downarrow}_{\mathbf{P}}\mathcal{A}
can be seen as a poset — it inherits the order
from
\mathbf{P}
.

Every object
B
of a
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
can be transformed into a poset
\mathsf{Po}(B)

whose points are the pairs
(u,b)
in which
u\in\mathbf{D}
and
b\in B(u)
. For
example:

B=\left(\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern 15.22223pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern-15.22223pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{1,2\}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-15.22223pt\raise-29.98352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\{5,6\}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern-13.97227pt\raise-14.99176pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}1\mapsto 5\\
2\mapsto 6\end{smallmatrix}}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-21.98352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 117.06473pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\_2,4)}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 79.58533pt\raise-59.96704pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{(\_1,7)}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 113.18614pt\raise-29.98352pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 102.44644pt\raise-51.96704pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}}}$}

The operation that does the inverse of ‘
\mathsf{Po}
’ is called ‘
\mathsf{Ob}
’. For
every object
B
in a
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
there is a bijection between the subsets
of
B
in
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
and the down-sets of
\mathsf{Po}(B)
. Let’s define:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}{\downarrow}(b\in B(u))&:=&\mathsf{Ob}({\downarrow}_{\mathsf{Po}(B)}(u,b)),\\[5.0pt]
\text{so:}\phantom{mm}{\downarrow}(1\in B(2\_))&=&\mathsf{Ob}({\downarrow}_{\mathsf{Po}(B)}(2\_,1))\\
&=&\mathsf{Ob}\left(\begin{smallmatrix}(2\_,1)\\
\downarrow&\searrow\\
(1\_,5)&&(\_1,7)\end{smallmatrix}\right)\\
&=&\left(\begin{smallmatrix}\{1\}&&\emptyset\\
\downarrow&\searrow&\downarrow\\
\{5\}&&\{7\}\end{smallmatrix}\right).\\
\end{array}

We will also use this other shorthand: if
u\in\mathbf{D}
, then

{\downarrow}u\;=\;{\downarrow}(*\in 1_{\mathbf{E}}(u)).

This looks ambiguous. We have both

\begin{array}[]{rl}&{\downarrow}2\_\;=\;{\downarrow}(*\in 1_{\mathbf{E}}(2\_))\;=\;{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}\;=\;21\;\subset\;1_{\mathbf{E}}\\
\text{and}&{\downarrow}2\_\;=\;{\downarrow}_{\mathbf{D}}\{2\_\}\;=\;\left\{\begin{smallmatrix}2\_,&\\
1\_,&\_1\\
\end{smallmatrix}\right\}\;\subset\;\mathbf{D}\,,\end{array}

but will always use the first meaning.

4.3
The classifier
\Omega

The classifier object on a
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
is the object
\Omega\in\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
whose
action on objects is
\Omega(u)=\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}u)
and whose action on
morphisms is
\Omega\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
\downarrow\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\right)(\mathcal{S})=\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v
. Let’s decypher this
— it contains an abuse of language.

Let
\mathbf{D}=\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)
and
u=2\_
. Then
{\downarrow}2\_={\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}\subset 1_{\mathbf{E}}
. We will convert that
{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}
to a poset

{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\cdot}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}
, and
\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}2\_)
will be the set of
down-sets of this new poset, which is:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}2\_)&=&\left\{{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\cdot}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}},\;{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\cdot}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}},\;{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\cdot}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}},\;{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\cdot}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}},\;{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\cdot}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right\}\\
&=&\{00,01,10,11,21\}\\
&=&\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-0.5,-0.5)\put(1.0,4.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${21}$\hss}}\put(2.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(1.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${11}$\hss}}\put(3.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${10}$\hss}}\put(2.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${01}$\hss}}\put(1.0,0.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${00}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)\\[10.0pt]
&\subset&H\\
\end{array}

Each ‘
\cdot
’ means “this is out of the domain”, but the precise
details vary according to the context.

If we do the same as above for
1\_
,
\_1
, and
\_2
, we get:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}2\_)={\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-0.5,-0.5)\put(1.0,4.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${21}$\hss}}\put(2.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(1.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${11}$\hss}}\put(3.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${10}$\hss}}\put(2.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${01}$\hss}}\put(1.0,0.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${00}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)}&&\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}\_2)={\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-0.5,-0.5)\put(1.0,4.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(2.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(1.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(3.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${02}$\hss}}\put(0.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(2.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${01}$\hss}}\put(1.0,0.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${00}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)}\\
\\
\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}1\_)={\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-0.5,-0.5)\put(1.0,4.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(2.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(1.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(3.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${10}$\hss}}\put(2.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(1.0,0.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${00}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)}&&\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}\_1)={\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-0.5,-0.5)\put(1.0,4.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(2.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(1.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(3.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(2.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${01}$\hss}}\put(1.0,0.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${00}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)}\\
\end{array}

When
\mathbf{D}=\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)
we can draw the classifier
of
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
as:

\Omega\;\;=\;{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-15.0,29.0)\put(15.0,75.0){\vector{15}{-15}{15.0}}\put(0.0,74.0){\vector{0}{-1}{13.0}}\put(45.0,74.0){\vector{0}{-1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,45.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-0.5,-0.5)\put(1.0,4.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(2.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(1.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(3.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${10}$\hss}}\put(2.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(1.0,0.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${00}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}}$\hss}}\put(0.0,90.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${{\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-0.5,-0.5)\put(1.0,4.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${21}$\hss}}\put(2.0,3.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(1.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${11}$\hss}}\put(3.0,2.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${\cdot}$\hss}}\put(0.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${10}$\hss}}\put(2.0,1.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${01}$\hss}}\put(1.0,0.0){\lower 1.25pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\tiny${00}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}}$\hss}}\put(45.0,45.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox When
\mathbf{D}
is a 2-column graph the classifier
\Omega\in\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
can always be
drawn in this (nice) way.

We still need to understand what the action of
\Omega
on morphisms
“means”. We can use the definition to calculate a particular case,

\begin{array}[]{ccl}\Omega\left(\begin{smallmatrix}u\\
\downarrow\\
v\end{smallmatrix}\right)(\mathcal{S})&=&\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v\\[8.0pt]
\Omega\left(\begin{smallmatrix}2\_\\
\downarrow\\
1\_\end{smallmatrix}\right)(11)&=&11\cap{\downarrow}1\_\\
&=&11\cap 10\;=\;10\\
\end{array}

and can use a diagram like the one in
[
FavC

, but
with the particular case in its right half, to put that in a more

4.4
The ‘true’ map
\top:1\hookrightarrow\Omega

The ‘true’ map
\top:1\hookrightarrow\Omega
will not be an inclusion if we take
1
as
the default terminal. We can fix this by defining
1_{\top}
as the
terminal that takes each
u\in\mathbf{D}
to
\{{\downarrow}u\}
, and by using this as
our ‘true’ map:
\top:1_{\top}\hookrightarrow\Omega
. Our
default
meaning for
1
is
still the terminal that takes each
u
to
\{*\}
, but in contexts
like “
\top:1\hookrightarrow\Omega
” the default meaning for the ‘1’ will change to
‘
1_{\top}
’.

When
\mathbf{D}=\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)

4.5
The classifying map
\chi_{f}
of an inclusion
f

Suppose that this diagram is a pullback in a
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
:

\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{1_{\top}}
\scriptstyle{!}
\textstyle{A}
\textstyle{B}
\scriptstyle{f}
\textstyle{1_{\top}}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\top}
\textstyle{B}
\textstyle{\Omega}
\scriptstyle{\chi_{f}}

then for every
u\in\mathbf{D}
this is also a pullback:

\textstyle{A(u)}
\textstyle{1_{\top}(u)}
\scriptstyle{!}
\textstyle{A(u)}
\textstyle{B(u)}
\scriptstyle{f_{u}}
\textstyle{1_{\top}(u)}
\textstyle{\Omega(u)}
\scriptstyle{\top_{u}}
\textstyle{B(u)}
\textstyle{\Omega(u)}
\scriptstyle{(\chi_{f})_{u}}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{{\downarrow}u}
\textstyle{{\downarrow}u}
\textstyle{{\downarrow}u}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{a}
\textstyle{(\chi_{f})_{u}(a)}

so, for all
u\in\mathbf{D}
:

A(u)\;=\;\{\,b\in B(u)\;|\;(\chi_{f})_{u}(b)={\downarrow}u\,\}

This gives us an elementary way to start from just
\chi_{f}:B\rightarrow\Omega
and
construct the pullback in a way that makes the left wall an inclusion.

The other direction is harder. Suppose that we have an inclusion
f:A\hookrightarrow B
in a
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
, and we want to construct the map
\chi_{f}:B\rightarrow\Omega
that
completes the pullback. For every
u\in\mathbf{D}
and
b\in B(u)
we will define:

\chi_{f}(b\in B(u))\;=\;\mathsf{CST}(A\cap{\downarrow}(b\in B(u)))

The operation
\mathsf{CST}
is new: it “canonicalizes subobjects of the
terminal”. If
C\in\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
, then
C
is a subobject of the terminal iff
the (unique) map
!_{C}:C\rightarrow 1
is a monic.
\mathsf{CST}(C)
is defined if an
only if
C
is a subobject of the terminal, and is defined as
\mathsf{CST}(C):=\operatorname{dom}(\mathsf{can}(!_{C}))
. Here are a diagram for the general case and an
example:

\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{\mathsf{CST}(C)}
\textstyle{C}
\textstyle{1_{\mathbf{E}}}
\scriptstyle{!_{C}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{CST}(C)}
\textstyle{1_{\mathbf{E}}}
\scriptstyle{\mathsf{can}(!_{C})}
\textstyle{{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-8.0,12.0)\put(8.0,28.56){\vector{9}{-6.12}{9.0}}\put(0.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(25.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(0.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{5\}}$\hss}}\put(0.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\emptyset}$\hss}}\put(25.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{6\}}$\hss}}\put(25.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{4\}}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}}

\scriptstyle{\chi_{f}}

It
does not
define the map
\chi_{f}
, but we can use the formula
above to calculate
\chi_{f}(b\in B(u))
for each pair
(u,b)
with
u\in\mathbf{D}
and

b\in B(u)
. Let’s do the case
u=2\_
,
b=2
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\chi_{f}(b\in B(u))&=&\mathsf{CST}(A\cap{\downarrow}(b\in B(u)))\\
\chi_{f}(2\in B(2\_))&=&\mathsf{CST}(A\cap{\downarrow}(2\in B(2\_)))\\
&=&\mathsf{CST}({\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-8.0,12.0)\put(8.0,28.56){\vector{9}{-6.12}{9.0}}\put(0.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(25.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(0.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{5\}}$\hss}}\put(0.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\emptyset}$\hss}}\put(25.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{6\}}$\hss}}\put(25.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{4\}}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}\cap{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-8.0,12.0)\put(8.0,28.56){\vector{9}{-6.12}{9.0}}\put(0.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(25.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(0.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{5\}}$\hss}}\put(0.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{2\}}$\hss}}\put(25.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{6\}}$\hss}}\put(25.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\emptyset}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)})\\
&=&\mathsf{CST}({\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-8.0,12.0)\put(8.0,28.56){\vector{9}{-6.12}{9.0}}\put(0.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(25.0,29.0){\vector{0}{-1}{7.0}}\put(0.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{5\}}$\hss}}\put(0.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\emptyset}$\hss}}\put(25.0,17.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\{6\}}$\hss}}\put(25.0,34.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\emptyset}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)})\\
&=&{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${1}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${0}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}\right)}\\
&=&11.\end{array}
