4.6
The canonical Grothendieck topology
{J_{\mathrm{can}}}

Here’s how to define the canonical Grothendieck topology on a
topological space
(X,\mathcal{O}(X))
. We will denote open sets of

\mathcal{O}(X)
by letters like
U,V,W
, and sets of open sets by letters
like
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{U}
— so
U,V,W\in\mathcal{O}(X)
and
\mathcal{A},\mathcal{S},\mathcal{U}\subset\mathcal{O}(X)
. We
will regard
\mathcal{O}(X)
as a downward-directed poset, so these are
equivalent:

\begin{matrix}U\\
\mathop{\textsf{above}}\\
V\end{matrix}\;\;,\qquad\begin{matrix}U\\
\downarrow\\
V\end{matrix}\;\;,\quad\text{and}\quad\begin{matrix}U\\
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subseteq$}\\
V\end{matrix}\;\;.

A subset
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{O}(X)
will be called a
sieve
if it is
downward-closed. A sieve
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{O}(X)
will be called a
sieve on

U
if
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{O}(U)
. We say that a sieve
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{O}(U)

covers

U
, or is a
covering sieve on

U
, if
\bigcup\mathcal{S}=U
, and we
say that a sieve
\mathcal{S}

r-covers

U
if its restriction to

\mathcal{O}(U)
,
\mathcal{S}\cap\mathcal{O}(U)
, covers
U
.

The set of all sieves on
U
will be denoted by
\Omega(U)
, and the set of
all covering sieves on
U
will be denoted by
{J_{\mathrm{can}}}(U)
. Some extra
notational conventions: The letters
\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{R}
will always denote
sieves, and
\mathcal{U}
will always denote a covering sieve (on
U
).

This diagram shows all these notional conventions, plus a few more:

\begin{matrix}U&\in&\mathcal{O}(X)\\
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subset$}&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subset$}\\
V&\in&\mathcal{S}&\in&\Omega(U)&=&\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}U)&\subset&\mathcal{P}({\downarrow}U)\\
&&&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subset$}&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subset$}\\
W&\in&\mathcal{U}&\in&{J_{\mathrm{can}}}(U)&=&\hbox to35.0pt{$\{\,\mathcal{S}\in\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}U)\;|\;\bigcup\mathcal{S}=U\,\}$\hss}\\
&&&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subset$}&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subset$}\\
&&&&1_{\top}(U)&=&\hbox to35.0pt{$\{\,\mathcal{S}\in\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}U)\;|\;\mathcal{S}={\downarrow}U\,\}$\hss}\\
\end{matrix}

This
{J_{\mathrm{can}}}
has this three properties:

\begin{array}[]{rccc}\mathsf{hasmax}_{J_{\mathrm{can}}}:&\forall U\in\mathcal{O}(X).&&{\downarrow}U\mathop{\textsf{covers}}U\;,\\[7.5pt]
\mathsf{stab}_{J_{\mathrm{can}}}:&\forall\left(\begin{matrix}U\\
\mathop{\textsf{above}}\\
V\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathcal{O}(X).&\forall\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(U).&\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{covers}}U\\
\downarrow\\
\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{r-covers}}V\\
\end{matrix}\right)\;,\\[22.0pt]
\mathsf{trans}_{J_{\mathrm{can}}}:&\forall U\in\mathcal{O}(X).\forall\mathcal{U}\in J(U).&\forall\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(U).&\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{covers}}U\\
\uparrow\\
\forall V\in\mathcal{U}.\;\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{r-covers}}V\\
\end{matrix}\right)\;.\\
\end{array}

4.7
Other Grothendieck topologies

Here’s how to define what is a Grothendieck topology on an arbitrary
downward-directed posed
\mathbf{D}
. The topology
\mathcal{O}(X)
of the previous
case will become the poset
\mathbf{D}
; we will refer to this
\mathbf{D}
as our
ex-topology
and to the points
u,v,w\in\mathbf{D}
as
ex-open sets
.
These are equivalent:

\begin{matrix}u\\
\mathop{\textsf{above}}\\
v\end{matrix}\qquad\text{and}\qquad\begin{matrix}u\\
\downarrow\\
v\end{matrix}\;\;.

We will denote ex-open sets by letters like
u
,
v
,
w
, and sets of
ex-open sets by letters like
\mathcal{A}
,
\mathcal{S}
, and
\mathcal{U}
. A subset
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbf{D}
will be
called a
sieve
if it is downward-closed. A sieve
\mathcal{S}\subset\mathbf{D}
will be
called a
sieve on

u
if
\mathcal{S}\subset{\downarrow}u
. The down-sets
\mathcal{O}(U)
of
the previous section will be replaced by
{\downarrow}u
here.

In this context the set of all sieves is exactly
H
(see
sec.
4
) and the set of all sieves on
u
is exactly
\Omega(u)

(see sec.
4.3
).

A
Grothendieck topology
is an object
J\in\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
obeying
1_{\top}\subset J\subset\Omega

and having the properties
\mathsf{hasmax}_{J}
,
\mathsf{stab}_{J}
, and
\mathsf{trans}_{J}
, that
are defined as:

\begin{array}[]{rccc}\mathsf{hasmax}_{J}:&\forall u\in\mathbf{D}.&&{\downarrow}u\mathop{\textsf{$J$-covers}}u\;,\\[7.5pt]
\mathsf{stab}_{J}:&\forall\left(\begin{matrix}u\\
\mathop{\textsf{above}}\\
v\end{matrix}\right)\in\mathbf{D}.&\forall\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u).&\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{$J$-covers}}u\\
\downarrow\\
\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{r-$J$-covers}}v\\
\end{matrix}\right)\;,\\[22.0pt]
\mathsf{trans}_{J}:&\forall u\in\mathbf{D}.\forall\mathcal{U}\in J(u).&\forall\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u).&\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{$J$-covers}}u\\
\uparrow\\
\forall v\in\mathcal{U}.\;\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{r-$J$-covers}}v\\
\end{matrix}\right)\;.\\
\end{array}

Here we say that a sieve
\mathcal{S}
on
u

J
-covers

u
if
\mathcal{S}\in J(u)
,
and that a sieve
\mathcal{S}

r-
J
-covers

v
if its restriction to

{\downarrow}v
,
\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v
,
J
-covers
v
— i.e., if
\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v\in J(v)
.

Here our notational conventions are that
\mathcal{R}
,
\mathcal{S}
, and
\mathcal{U}
are sieves,
and that
\mathcal{U}
is a
J
-covering sieve on
u
. Other calligraphic
capitals, like
\mathcal{A}
, may denote subsets of
\mathbf{D}_{0}
that don’t need to be
downward-closed.

This diagram shows all these notional conventions, plus a few more:

\begin{matrix}u&\in&\mathbf{D}\\
\downarrow&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subset$}\\
v&\in&\mathcal{S}&\in&\Omega(u)&=&\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}u)&\subset&\mathcal{P}({\downarrow}u)\\
&&&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subset$}\\
w&\in&\mathcal{U}&\in&J(u)\\
&&&&\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subset$}\\
&&&&1_{\top}(u)&=&\hbox to35.0pt{$\{\,\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u)\;|\;\mathcal{S}={\downarrow}u\,\}$\hss}\\
\end{matrix}

4.8
Every
J(u)
is a filter

Remember that if
\mathbf{P}
is an upward-directed poset with terminal object

\top
and binary meet
\land
then a subset
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{P}
is a filter if it contains

\top
and is closed upwards and by binary meets. More formally,
\mathbf{F}
is a
filter in
\mathbf{P}
if:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}&\phantom{mmmmmm}\top\in\mathbf{F},\\[5.0pt]
\forall\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\in\mathbf{P}.&\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{S}\\
\mathop{\textsf{above}}\\
\mathcal{R}\end{matrix}\right)\rightarrow\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{S}\in\mathbf{F}\\
\uparrow\\
\mathcal{R}\in\mathbf{F}\end{matrix}\right),\\[20.0pt]
\forall\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\in\mathbf{P}.&\phantom{mmmmm}\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\in\mathbf{F}\\
\downarrow\\
\mathcal{R}\land\mathcal{S}\in\mathbf{F}\end{matrix}\right).\\
\end{array}

Also, a filter
\mathbf{F}\subset\mathbf{P}
is
principal
when it contains the meet of
all its elements; when this happens we have
\mathbf{F}={\uparrow}_{\mathbf{P}}\{\bigwedge\mathbf{F}\}={\uparrow}\bigwedge\mathbf{F}
, and we say that
\mathbf{F}
is generated by its bottom
element
\bigwedge\mathbf{F}
. When
\mathbf{P}
is a finite poset all the filters on

\mathbf{P}
are principal.

Theorem 4.8.1.

If
J
is a Grothendieck topology on a
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
then every
J(u)
is a
filter on
\Omega(u)
.

Proof.
We can re-state this as 1)
J(u)
contains the top
element of
\Omega(u)
, 2)
J(u)
is closed upwards, 3)
J(u)
is closed by
binary meets, and we can re-state that again in a more visual way as:

\begin{array}[]{rccc}1)&&\phantom{mmmmm}{\downarrow}u\in J(u),\\[5.0pt]
2)&\forall\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u).&\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{S}\\
\rotatebox{90.0}{$\subset$}\\
\mathcal{R}\end{matrix}\right)\rightarrow\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{S}\in J(u)\\
\uparrow\\
\mathcal{R}\in J(u)\end{matrix}\right),\\[20.0pt]
3)&\forall\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u).&\phantom{mmmmm}\left(\begin{matrix}\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S}\in J(u)\\
\downarrow\\
\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S}\in J(u)\end{matrix}\right).\\
\end{array}

Part (1) is an obvious consequence of
\{{\downarrow}u\}=1_{\top}(u)\subset J(u)\subset\Omega(u)
. The proofs of (2) and (3) are quite technical and difficult to
understand intuitively, so we will present them in Natural Deduction
form and let the reader check that every step is correct.

This is the proof that
J(u)
is upwards-closed:

\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\in J(u)\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\in J(u)\mathstrut\mathcal{S}\in J(u)\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut[\mathcal{R}\in J(u)]^{2}\mathstrut\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u)\mathstrut\hskip-128.0374pt\mathstrut\forall v\in\mathcal{R}.\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{r-$J$-covers}}v\mathstrut\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{r-$J$-covers}}v\mathstrut\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v\in J(v)\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v={\downarrow}v\mathstrut{\downarrow}v\subset\mathcal{S}\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut v\in\mathcal{S}\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut[v\in\mathcal{R}]^{1}\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{S}\mathstrut\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u)\mathstrut{\downarrow}v\in J(v)\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut v\in\mathbf{D}\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut[v\in\mathcal{R}]^{1}\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\subset\mathbf{D}\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\in\Omega(u)\mathstrut\mathsf{hasmax}_{J}\mathstrut\hskip-99.58464pt\mathstrut\mathsf{trans}_{J}

And this is the proof that
J(u)
is closed by binary meets:

\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S}\in J(u)\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\in J(u)\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u)\mathstrut\hskip-113.81102pt\mathstrut\forall v\in\mathcal{R}.(\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S})\mathop{\textsf{r-$J$-covers}}v\mathstrut\forall v\in\mathcal{R}.(\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S})\cap{\downarrow}v\in J(v)\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut\forall v\in\mathcal{R}.\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v\in J(v)\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\subset{\downarrow}u\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\in J(u)\mathstrut\forall v\in{\downarrow}u.\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v\in J(v)\mathstrut\forall v\in{\downarrow}u.\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{r-$J$-covers}}v\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut\mathcal{S}\mathop{\textsf{$J$-covers}}u\mathstrut\mathcal{S}\in J(u)\mathstrut\mathsf{stab}_{J}\mathstrut\forall v\in\mathcal{R}.\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v=(\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S})\cap{\downarrow}v\mathstrut\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v=(\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S})\cap{\downarrow}v\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v=\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}v\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\cap{\downarrow}v={\downarrow}v\mathstrut{\downarrow}v\subset\mathcal{R}\lx@proof@logical@and\mathstrut{\downarrow}v\subset{\downarrow}\mathcal{R}\mathstrut[v\in\mathcal{R}]^{1}\mathstrut{\downarrow}\mathcal{R}=\mathcal{R}\mathstrut\mathcal{R}\in J(u)\mathstrut\hskip-99.58464pt\mathstrut\mathsf{trans}_{J}

Remember that we established (in sec.
4
) that
\mathbf{D}
always
stands for a
finite
downward-directed poset. So in a
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
all
‘
\Omega(u)
’s are finite upward-directed posets, and all filters on each

\Omega(u)
are principal and can recovered from their bottom elements.
This gives us a very compact way to represent Grothendieck topologies.
For example, if
\mathbf{D}=\left({\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-3.0,1.0)\put(3.0,14.0){\vector{6}{-4}{6.0}}\put(0.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(0.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,8.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\put(12.0,16.0){\lower 2.0pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss\scriptsize${\bullet}$\hss}}\end{picture}}}}\right)
and

\begin{array}[]{lll}\bigwedge J(2\_)=10,&&\bigwedge J(\_2)=00,\\
\bigwedge J(1\_)=10,&&\bigwedge J(\_1)=00\\
\end{array}

then
1_{\top}\subset J\subset\Omega(u)
is:

{\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-15.0,29.0)\put(15.0,75.0){\vector{15}{-15}{15.0}}\put(0.0,74.0){\vector{0}{-1}{13.0}}\put(45.0,74.0){\vector{0}{-1}{13.0}}\put(0.0,45.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox 4.9
Every closure operator
\overline{(\cdot)}
induces a nucleus
(\cdot)^{*}

In this section we will suppose that our topos
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}=\mathbf{E}
has a closure
operator
\overline{(\cdot)}
.

If
\mathcal{S}
is a truth-value in
\mathbf{E}
then we will denote the closure of
\mathcal{S}\hookrightarrow 1
by
\mathcal{S}^{*}\hookrightarrow 1
, and if
\mathcal{R}
is a truth-value “smaller than

\mathcal{S}
”, in the sense that we have an inclusion
\mathcal{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
, then we will
denote the closure of the inclusion
\mathcal{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
by
\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
.
Formally,

0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 83.27pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{R}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 110.79518pt\raise-29.98352pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 93.67743pt\raise-5.5pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 1.76776pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\textstyle{}$}}}}}{\hbox{\kern 3.53554pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise-3.53554pt\hbox{\lx@xy@hook{1}}}}}}}}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 100.23785pt\raise-13.28563pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 111.03053pt\raise-21.07126pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@drawline@}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces}}}}}\qquad\begin{array}[]{rccl}(\cdot)^{*}:&H&\rightarrow&H\\
&\mathcal{S}&\rightarrow&\operatorname{dom}(\overline{\mathsf{inc}(\mathcal{S},1)})\\
\\
(\cdot)^{\mathcal{S}}:&{\downarrow}\mathcal{S}&\rightarrow&{\downarrow}\mathcal{S}\\
&\mathcal{R}&\rightarrow&\operatorname{dom}(\overline{\mathsf{inc}(\mathcal{R},\mathcal{S})})\\
\end{array}

A
nucleus
on a Heyting Algebra
H
is an operation
(\cdot)^{*}:H\rightarrow H

that obeys M1, M2, and M3 below, and if
\mathcal{S}\in H
then an operation
(\cdot)^{\mathcal{S}}:{\downarrow}\mathcal{S}\rightarrow{\downarrow}\mathcal{S}
is a
modality on the down-set
{\downarrow}\mathcal{S}
if it obeys
MD1, MD2, and MD4 below:

\begin{array}[]{l}\text{M1) }\mathcal{S}\subset\mathcal{S}^{*},\\
\text{M2) }\mathcal{S}^{*}=\mathcal{S}^{**},\\
\text{M3) }\mathcal{R}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}^{*}=(\mathcal{R}\cap\mathcal{S})^{*},\\
\end{array}\qquad\begin{array}[]{l}\text{MD1) }\mathcal{R}\subset\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}},\\
\text{MD2) }\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}}=\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}\mathcal{S}},\\
\text{MD3) }\mathcal{Q}^{\mathcal{S}}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}}=(\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{R})^{\mathcal{S}}.\\
\end{array}

Theorem 4.9.1.

The operation
(\cdot)^{*}
induced by the closure operator
\overline{(\cdot)}
is a
modality on
H
, and the operation
(\cdot)^{\mathcal{S}}
is a modality on the
down-set
{\downarrow}\mathcal{S}
.

Proof.
We will only prove MD1, MD2, and MD3. The proofs for MD1
and MD2 are essentially the same as the proofs for C1 and C2 in
sec.
2
; the diagram is the one at the left below. The proof
of MD3 is based on the proof of C4 in sec.
2
. Let
c:\mathcal{Q}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
and
d:\mathcal{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
. We know from C4 that
\chi_{(\overline{c}\cap\overline{d})}
=

\chi_{\overline{c\cap d}}
, so
\operatorname{dom}(\overline{c}\cap\overline{d})=\mathcal{Q}^{\mathcal{S}}\cap\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}}
and
\operatorname{dom}(\overline{c\cap d})=(\mathcal{Q}\cap\mathcal{R})^{\mathcal{S}}
are
the same object.

\vbox{\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\kern-68.27824pt\hbox{{\hbox{\kern 68.27824pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\raise-2.5pt\hbox{$\textstyle{\mathcal{R}}$}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 4.10
Every nucleus
(\cdot)^{*}

The operations

Theorem 4.10.2.

a) If
f:\mathcal{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}={\downarrow}u
then
\chi_{f}(*\in\mathcal{S}(u))=(\mathcal{R}\in\Omega(u))
.

b) If
g:\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}={\downarrow}u
then
\chi_{g}(*\in\mathcal{S}(u))=(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}}\in\Omega(u))
.

c) If
h:\mathcal{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}={\downarrow}u
then
\chi_{\overline{h}}(*\in\mathcal{S}(u))=(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}}\in\Omega(u))
.

d) If
h:\mathcal{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}={\downarrow}u
then
(j\circ\chi_{h})(*\in\mathcal{S}(u))=(\mathcal{R}^{\mathcal{S}}\in\Omega(u))
.

e) If
h:\mathcal{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}={\downarrow}u
then
(j\circ\chi_{h})(*\in\mathcal{S}(u))=(\mathcal{R}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u))
.

f) If
h:\mathcal{R}\hookrightarrow\mathcal{S}
and
\mathcal{S}={\downarrow}u
then
j(\mathcal{R}\in\Omega(u))=(\mathcal{R}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u))
.

g) If
\mathcal{S}={\downarrow}u
then
j(\mathcal{R}\in\Omega(u))=(\mathcal{R}^{*}\cap\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u))
.

h)
j(\mathcal{R}\in\Omega(u))=(\mathcal{R}^{*}\cap{\downarrow}u\in\Omega(u))
.

i)
j(u)(\mathcal{R})=\mathcal{R}^{*}\cap{\downarrow}u
.

j)
j=\lambda u\in\mathbf{D}.\lambda\mathcal{R}\in\Omega(u).(\mathcal{R}^{*}\land{\downarrow}u)
.

Proof.
Item (a) is a consequence of the formula in section

4.5
:

\begin{array}[]{rcl}\chi_{f}(b\in B(u))&=&(\mathsf{CST}(A\cap({\downarrow}(b\in B(u)))\in\Omega(u))\\[5.0pt]
\chi_{f}(*\in\mathcal{S}(u))&=&(\mathsf{CST}(\mathcal{R}\cap({\downarrow}(*\in\mathcal{S}(u)))\in\Omega(u))\\
&=&(\mathsf{CST}(\mathcal{R}\cap{\downarrow}u)\in\Omega(u))\\
&=&(\mathsf{CST}(\mathcal{R})\in\Omega(u))\\
&=&(\mathcal{R}\in\Omega(u))\\
\end{array}

\textstyle{(*\in\mathcal{S}(u))}
\textstyle{(\mathcal{R}\in\Omega(u))}

4.11
Some bijections

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a finite 2-column graph. As usual, let
\mathbf{E}
be the topos

\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
, and let
H=\mathsf{Subsets}(1_{\mathbf{E}})
be its Heyting Algebra of
truth-values. Let’s denote the set of subsets of
\mathbf{D}_{0}
by
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{D})
,
the set of nuclei on
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
by
\mathsf{Nucs}(\mathbf{D})
, the set of Grotendieck
topologies on
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
by
\mathsf{GrTops}(\mathbf{D})
, the set of Lawvere-Tierney
topologies on
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
by
\mathsf{LTTops}(\mathbf{D})
, and the set of closure
operators on
\mathbf{Set}^{\mathbf{D}}
by
\mathsf{Clops}(\mathbf{D})
.

We have bijections between the sets
\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{D})
,
\mathsf{Nucs}(\mathbf{D})
, and

\mathsf{GrTops}(\mathbf{D})
. They are proved, together with the commutatity of the
triangle, in
[
Lin14
]
and
[
Och21
]
. The
bijection between
\mathsf{GrTops}(\mathbf{D})
and
\mathsf{LTTops}(\mathbf{D})
is proved in

[
LM92
]
, theorem V.1.2 and section V.4, and the
bijection between
\mathsf{LTTops}(\mathbf{D})
and
\mathsf{Clops}(\mathbf{D})
has brief proofs in
several standard books and a detailed proof in sections

2
–
3
here.

The diagram below shows all these bijections and where more
information about them can be found. The right half shows how we will
refer to the here; for example,
(J\leftrightarrow j)
is the bijection between

\mathsf{GrTops}(\mathbf{D})
and
\mathsf{LTTops}(\mathbf{D})
, and we call its components
(J\mapsto j)
and

(j\mapsto J)
.

\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{D})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Nucs}(\mathbf{D})}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\text{C.4.2,\;C.2,}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{D})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Nucs}(\mathbf{D})}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LindenhoviusOchs}{}{}]}}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{D})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{GrTops}(\mathbf{D})}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\text{2.8,}\\
\text{C.4.1}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{P}(\mathbf{D})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{GrTops}(\mathbf{D})}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\text{2.9}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{GrTops}(\mathbf{D})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Nucs}(\mathbf{D})}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\begin{smallmatrix}\text{B.8,}\\
\text{B.25}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{GrTops}(\mathbf{D})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{LTTops}(\mathbf{D})}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{MacLaneMoerdijk}{}{}]}}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathsf{LTTops}(\mathbf{D})}
\textstyle{\mathsf{Clops}(\mathbf{D})}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}\text{[here]}\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}
\textstyle{(\cdot)^{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\begin{smallmatrix}(\mathcal{Y}\mapsto(\cdot)^{*}),\\
((\cdot)^{*}\mapsto\mathcal{Y})\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{\mathcal{Y}}
\textstyle{J}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\begin{smallmatrix}(\mathcal{Y}\mapsto J),\\
(J\mapsto\mathcal{Y})\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{J}
\textstyle{(\cdot)^{*}}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\begin{smallmatrix}((\cdot)^{*}\mapsto J),\\
(J\mapsto(\cdot)^{*})\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{J}
\textstyle{j}
\textstyle{\scriptstyle\begin{smallmatrix}(J\mapsto j),\\
(j\mapsto J)\end{smallmatrix}}
\textstyle{j}
\textstyle{\overline{(\cdot)}}
\scriptstyle{\begin{smallmatrix}(j\mapsto\overline{(\cdot)}),\\
(\overline{(\cdot)}\mapsto j)\end{smallmatrix}}

These are the components of the bijections in the triangle. The
references like “C.4.2” point to sections, theorems, and definitions
in
[
Lin14
]
.

\begin{array}[]{rrcll}(\mathcal{Y}\mapsto(\cdot)^{*}):&\mathcal{S}^{*}&=&\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}&\text{C.4.2, C.2}\\
((\cdot)^{*}\mapsto\mathcal{Y}):&\mathcal{Y}&=&\{\,u\in\mathbf{D}\;|\;({\downarrow}u)^{*}\not=({\downarrow}^{\circ}u)^{*}\,\}&\text{\cite[cite]{[\@@bibref{}{LindenhoviusOchs}{}{}]}}\\[5.0pt]
(\mathcal{Y}\mapsto J):&J(u)&=&\{\,\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u)\;|\;\mathcal{Y}\cap{\downarrow}u\subseteq\mathcal{S}\,\}&\text{2.8, C.4.1}\\
(J\mapsto\mathcal{Y}):&X_{J}&=&\{\,u\in\mathbf{D}\;|\;J(u)=\{{\downarrow}u\}\,\}&\text{2.9}\\[5.0pt]
((\cdot)^{*}\mapsto J):&J(u)&=&\{\,\mathcal{S}\in\Omega(u)\;|\;u\in\mathcal{S}^{*}\,\}&\text{B.8, B.25}\\
(J\mapsto(\cdot)^{*}):&\mathcal{S}^{*}&=&\{\,u\in\mathbf{D}\;|\;\mathcal{S}\cap{\downarrow}u\in J(u)\,\}&\text{B.8, B.25}\\
\end{array}

These are the components of the bijection
(J\leftrightarrow j)
:

\begin{array}[]{rrcll}(J\mapsto j):&j&=&\chi_{(J\hookrightarrow\Omega)}\\
(j\mapsto J):&J&=&\operatorname{dom}(\sigma(j))\\
\end{array}

These are the components of the bijection
(j\leftrightarrow\overline{(\cdot)})
:

\begin{array}[]{rrcll}(j\mapsto\overline{(\cdot)}):&\overline{(f:A\hookrightarrow B)}&=&\sigma(j\circ\chi_{f})\\
(\overline{(\cdot)}\mapsto j):&j&=&\chi_{\overline{(1_{\top}\hookrightarrow\Omega)}}\\
\end{array}

We have also defined these two operations in sections

4.9
and
4.10
:

\begin{array}[]{rrcll}(\overline{(\cdot)}\mapsto(\cdot)^{*}):&\mathcal{S}^{*}&=&\operatorname{dom}(\overline{(\mathcal{S}\hookrightarrow 1)})\\
((\cdot)^{*}\mapsto j):&j(u)(\mathcal{R})&=&\mathcal{R}^{*}\cap{\downarrow}u\\
\end{array}

We did not prove that our formulas for them yield exactly the
corresponding components of the bijections obtained by composing the
bijections that we know. Let state that as two conjectures:

Conjecture 4.11.1.
((\cdot)^{*}\mapsto j)=((\cdot)^{*}\mapsto J);(J\mapsto j)
.

Conjecture 4.11.2.
(\overline{(\cdot)}\mapsto(\cdot)^{*})=(\overline{(\cdot)}\mapsto j);(j\mapsto J);(J\mapsto(\cdot)^{*})
.

4.12
Valid 4-uples

One way to start to get some visual intuition on what nuclei and
topologies “mean” is to choose a 2-column graph
\mathbf{D}
and then choose
either a
(\cdot)^{*}
, a
\mathcal{Y}
, a
J
, or a
j
on it, and then calculate the
other three elements of the 4-uple
((\cdot)^{*},\mathcal{Y},J,j)
from it using the
bijections from the previous section. We will always draw these
4-uples in this shape:

\begin{matrix}\mathcal{Y}&\leftrightarrow&(\cdot)^{*}\\
\updownarrow&\rotatebox{45.0}{$\leftrightarrow$}&\\
J&\leftrightarrow&j\\
\end{matrix}

The main result of
[
PH2
]
is a way to visualize the bijection

(\mathcal{Y}\leftrightarrow(\cdot)^{*})
when
\mathbf{D}
is a 2-column graph. We will need to change its
notation a bit. In
[
PH2
]
the notation for a 2CG with question
marks is
((P,A),Q)
and the notation for a ZHA with a nucleus is

(H,J)
; here we will use
((\mathbf{P}_{0},A),\mathcal{Q})
and
(H,(\cdot)^{*})
. The set of arrows
A

of the 2CG is a subset of
\mathbf{P}_{0}\times\mathbf{P}_{0}
, and
\mathcal{Q}
is the set of points of

\mathbf{P}_{0}
“with question marks”. Here is an example of a
((\mathbf{P}_{0},A),\mathcal{Q})
with its
associated
(H,(\cdot)^{*})
:

\begin{array}[]{ccc}\left(\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-20.0,18.0)\put(0.0,25.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${1\_}$\hss}}\put(0.0,50.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${2\_}$\hss}}\put(0.0,75.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${3\_}$\hss}}\put(0.0,100.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${4\_}$\hss}}\put(60.0,25.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\_1}$\hss}}\put(60.0,50.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\_2}$\hss}}\put(60.0,75.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\_3}$\hss}}\put(60.0,100.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\_4}$\hss}}\put(60.0,125.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\_5}$\hss}}\put(60.0,150.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\_6}$\hss}}\put(-15.0,50.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${?}$\hss}}\put(-15.0,75.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${?}$\hss}}\put(-15.0,100.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${?}$\hss}}\put(75.0,25.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${?}$\hss}}\put(75.0,50.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${?}$\hss}}\put(75.0,75.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${?}$\hss}}\put(75.0,125.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${?}$\hss}}\put(12.0,25.0){\vector{36}{0}{36.0}}\put(12.0,50.0){\vector{36}{0}{36.0}}\put(12.0,80.0){\vector{36}{15}{36.0}}\put(12.0,105.0){\vector{36}{15}{36.0}}\put(48.0,110.0){\vector{-36}{-45}{36.0}}\put(0.0,93.0){\vector{0}{-1}{11.0}}\put(0.0,68.0){\vector{0}{-1}{11.0}}\put(0.0,43.0){\vector{0}{-1}{11.0}}\put(60.0,143.0){\vector{0}{-1}{11.0}}\put(60.0,118.0){\vector{0}{-1}{11.0}}\put(60.0,93.0){\vector{0}{-1}{11.0}}\put(60.0,68.0){\vector{0}{-1}{11.0}}\put(60.0,43.0){\vector{0}{-1}{11.0}}\end{picture}}}\right)&\;\;{\begin{picture}(-5.2,-1.2)\polyline(-3,0)(0,0)(1,1)(3,-1)(5,1)(7,-1)(9,1)(11,-1)(12,0)(14,0)

\put(3.0,3.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${03}$\hss}}

\end{picture}}}\\[70.0pt]
((\mathbf{P}_{0},A),\mathcal{Q})&\;\;{\begin{picture}(-5.2,-1.2)\polyline(-3,0)(0,0)(1,1)(3,-1)(5,1)(7,-1)(9,1)(11,-1)(12,0)(14,0)
\polygon*(-5,0)(-2,1.0)(-2,-1.0)
\polygon*(17,0)(14,1.0)(14,-1.0)
\end{picture}}&(H,(\cdot)^{*})\\
\end{array}

We will regard two-digit numbers as “piles” of elements of
\mathbf{P}_{0}
:

ab\;\;=\;\;\mathsf{pile}(ab)\;\;=\;\;{\{a\_,\ldots,1\_,\;\_1,\ldots,\_b\}}

A set of question marks
\mathcal{Q}
induces an equivalence relation
\sim_{\mathcal{Q}}
on

H
: we have
ab\sim_{\mathcal{Q}}cd
when
ab
and
cd
become indistinguishable
when we erase the information on the question marks; formally,
ab\sim_{\mathcal{Q}}cd
means
\mathsf{pile}(ab)\backslash\mathcal{Q}=\mathsf{pile}(cd)\backslash\mathcal{Q}
. The operation
(\cdot)^{*}
takes
each
ab\in H
to the topmost element in its region — in the example
above we have
12^{*}=23
— and it also induces an equivalence
relation:
ab\sim_{(\cdot)^{*}}cd
when
ab^{*}=cd^{*}
. We say that a set of
question marks
\mathcal{Q}
“is associated to” a nucleus
(\cdot)^{*}
when
(\sim_{\mathcal{Q}})=(\sim_{(\cdot)^{*}})
.

Let’s now expand the definitions of the two components of the
bijection
(\mathcal{Y}\leftrightarrow(\cdot)^{*})
. The “Heyting Implication” ‘
\rightarrow
’ defined in

[
Lin14
, def.C.2]
is exactly the intuitionistic implication
from
[
PH1
, sec.16]
, that is calculated using the topological
interior, and
{\downarrow}^{\circ}u
is a shorthand for
{\downarrow}u\backslash\{u\}
. It turns out
that
\mathcal{Y}
is exactly the set of points of
\mathbf{P}_{0}
without questions
marks, i.e.,
\mathcal{Q}=\mathbf{P}_{0}\backslash\mathcal{Y}
and
\mathcal{Y}=\mathbf{P}_{0}\backslash\mathcal{Q}
:

\begin{array}[]{rrcll}(\mathcal{Y}\mapsto(\cdot)^{*}):&\mathcal{S}^{*}&=&\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow\mathcal{S}\\
&&=&{\operatorname{\mathsf{int}}}(\{\,u\in\mathbf{P}\;|\;u\in\mathcal{Y}\rightarrow u\in\mathcal{S}\,\})\\
&&=&{\operatorname{\mathsf{int}}}(\{\,u\in\mathbf{P}\;|\;u\not\in\mathcal{Y}\lor u\in\mathcal{S}\,\})\\
&&=&{\operatorname{\mathsf{int}}}(\{\,u\in\mathbf{P}\;|\;u\in(\mathbf{P}_{0}\backslash\mathcal{Y})\lor u\in\mathcal{S}\,\})\\
&&=&{\operatorname{\mathsf{int}}}(\{\,u\in\mathbf{P}\;|\;u\in(\mathbf{P}_{0}\backslash\mathcal{Y})\cup\mathcal{S}\,\})\\
&&=&{\operatorname{\mathsf{int}}}((\mathbf{P}_{0}\backslash\mathcal{Y})\cup\mathcal{S})\\
&&=&{\operatorname{\mathsf{int}}}(\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathcal{S})\\
((\cdot)^{*}\mapsto\mathcal{Y}):&\mathcal{Y}&=&\{\,u\in\mathbf{D}\;|\;({\downarrow}u)^{*}\not=({\downarrow}^{\circ}u)^{*}\,\}\\
\end{array}

We have:

\begin{array}[]{rrcll}(\mathcal{Q}\mapsto(\cdot)^{*}):&\mathcal{S}^{*}&=&{\operatorname{\mathsf{int}}}(\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathcal{S})\\
((\cdot)^{*}\mapsto\mathcal{Q}):&\mathcal{Q}&=&\{\,u\in\mathbf{D}\;|\;({\downarrow}u)^{*}=({\downarrow}^{\circ}u)^{*}\,\}\\
\end{array}

Let’s see some examples to make this more concrete. Suppose that
\mathcal{Q}

is the set of question marks of the example above. Then we can
calculate
12^{*}
by doing
{\operatorname{\mathsf{int}}}(\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathsf{pile}(12))
; the ‘
{\operatorname{\mathsf{int}}}
’ discards
the points
4\_
,
3\_
, and
\_5
from
\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathsf{pile}(12)
, and
12^{*}={\operatorname{\mathsf{int}}}(\mathcal{Q}\cup\mathsf{pile}(12))=\mathsf{pile}(23)=23
. Now let’s start by the
(\cdot)^{*}
of
the example and try to obtain
\mathcal{Q}
. Let
u=3\_
. Then
{\downarrow}u=34
,

{\downarrow}^{\circ}u=24
, and 34 and 24 are in the same region of the ZHA, so

3\_\in\mathcal{Q}
. Let’s try
u=\_4
. Then
{\downarrow}u=04
,
{\downarrow}^{\circ}u=03
, and 04 and 03
are in different regions the ZHA, so
\_4\not\in\mathcal{Q}
.

Using these methods we can easily convert a
\mathcal{Q}
to a
(\cdot)^{*}
and
vice-versa; but we want to use
\mathcal{Y}
instead of
\mathcal{Q}
in our 4-uples, and
we will draw our ‘
\mathcal{Y}
’s as subsets of
\mathbf{P}_{0}
, like this:

\left\{\begin{matrix}&\_6,\\
&\\
&\_4,\\
&\\
&\\
1\_&\\
\end{matrix}\right\}\;\;\>\lx@xy@svg{\hbox{\raise 2.5pt\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\hbox{\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.49313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 9.49313pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{{}\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 3.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{$\scriptstyle{}$}}}\kern 3.0pt}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\kern 24.98627pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\hbox{\kern 0.0pt\raise 0.0pt\hbox{\lx@xy@tip{1}\lx@xy@tip{-1}}}}}}\ignorespaces{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}{\hbox{\lx@xy@droprule}}\ignorespaces}}}}\>\;\;\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-1.0,-1.0)\Line(0,-1)(-1,0)

In our section
4.10
we saw that each component

j(u):\Omega(u)\rightarrow\Omega(u)
of a Lawvere-Tierney topology
j
is a modality on
the down-set
\Omega(u)=\mathsf{D}({\downarrow}u)
, and we have
j(u)(\mathcal{R})={\downarrow}u\land\mathcal{R}^{*}=\mathcal{R}^{{\downarrow}u}
; so each
j(u)
is “
(\cdot)^{*}
truncated to
\Omega(u)
”. We will
use this idea to draw our ‘
j
’s in a nice way — each component

j(u)
will be drawn as a slashing on the corresponding
\Omega(u)
, and we
will drawing ‘
\cdot
’s on all points of
H
that are “out of the
domain”, i.e., outside that
\Omega(u)
. Remember that at the end of our
section
4.8
we drew
1_{\top}\subset J\subset\Omega
as:

\end{picture}}}}$\hss}}\put(85.0,240.0){\lower 2.5pt\hbox to0.0pt{\hss${\vbox{\hbox{\begin{picture}(-2.5,-1.0)\Line(0,-1)(-1,0)

The diagram above is a particular case this one,

\begin{matrix}\mathcal{Y}&\leftrightarrow&(\cdot)^{*}\\
\updownarrow&\rotatebox{45.0}{$\leftrightarrow$}&\\
J&\leftrightarrow&j\\
\end{matrix}

in the sense that we replaced each expression in its corners by its
value on a certain particular case. If we draw many such particular
cases — by choosing a 2-column graph
\mathbf{D}
, then a nucleus
(\cdot)^{*}
as a
slashing, at then calculating the corresponding ‘
\mathcal{Y}
’s, ‘
j
’, and
‘
J
’s by hand or by computer, we will see that those same patterns
always occur: each element of
\mathcal{Y}
corresponds to one of the diagonal
cuts in the slashing of
(\cdot)^{*}
,
j
is always obtained by doing
truncations of
(\cdot)^{*}
, and each
J(u)
is made of the element in the
topmost equivalence class of the corresponding
j(u)
.
